#10609. 7 E-commerce UX Tips for Selling Car Accessories and Home & Kitchen Products Online

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Here is a question a product page should answer in about five seconds: “Will this magnetic phone mount hold my phone with the case still on?” When the answer is hard to find, shoppers tend to open another tab instead of contacting support.

Stores that sell both car accessories and home & kitchen products face an extra challenge: two very different catalogs share one site. A car accessories buyer cares about fit, mounting and compatibility. A home & kitchen buyer cares about size, capacity and how easy the product is to clean. A single generic product page frustrates both.

This guide covers seven practical e-commerce UX tips that designers and developers can apply right away, with code snippets and a checklist at the end.

A car mount and a vegetable peeler should not share the same spec table. Keep a single reusable product page component and change only the fields based on category. The layout stays consistent, while each shopper sees the details they need.

In code, this can be a config object that your template reads from:

Showing “Not specified” instead of hiding a missing field is a small habit with a real benefit: it makes gaps in your product data visible to your own team.

Shoppers look for deal-breaker information near the price and the purchase button: fit, size and compatibility. Do not bury it in an “Additional information” tab halfway down the page.

A short comparison table also works better than three paragraphs of marketing copy, because shoppers scan rather than read. Baymard Institute’s product page research consistently finds that unclear or hard-to-find specifications are a common reason shoppers hesitate.

A responsive layout is the starting point, not the finish line. People compare car accessories in a parking lot and browse kitchen gadgets while standing in the kitchen, often with one thumb.

A phone mount on a white background says nothing about how it attaches to a dashboard. Show it in a real car, from the driver’s point of view. For home & kitchen products, place the item next to something familiar, like a coffee mug, so people can judge its size.

Good alt text matters for accessibility and for image search. Compare “phone mount” with “Magnetic phone mount fixed to a car dashboard, holding a phone in landscape mode”. The W3C alt text decision tree is a useful guide for deciding what to describe.

Keep images fast, too. Responsive sources with srcset and sizes, explicit dimensions and lazy loading help prevent layout shift and slow pages:

One caveat: do not lazy-load the main product image at the top of the page. It is usually the Largest Contentful Paint element, so web.dev advises loading it eagerly.

Shoppers often search for a problem rather than a product. Few people type “seat gap filler” the first time; they type “stuff falling between car seats”. Your search, synonyms and category names should handle both.

Mixed catalogs show why this matters. Stores that cover both vehicle and household products, such as VNSPORT.org with its separate product collections, keep the two audiences apart so that someone looking for a dashboard mount never has to scroll past kitchen tools. The principle applies to any store with more than one audience: group by the shopper’s intent, not by your supplier list.

Decorative “100% secure” badges do little. What builds trust is information shoppers can check for themselves:

At checkout, show the full cost, including shipping and taxes, before payment, let people fix mistakes easily and offer guest checkout. In Baymard’s checkout usability research, unexpected extra costs and forced account creation are among the top reasons shoppers abandon a cart.

If your support inbox gets the same question every week, the product page has a gap. Answer it on the page, close to where the decision happens, instead of on a generic help page three clicks away.

You can mark up those answers with FAQPage structured data:

A reality check: since 2023, Google shows FAQ rich results only for well-known government and health websites. Do not add FAQs just to chase a snippet. Add them because they answer real questions and help shoppers decide.

What are the most important e-commerce UX tips for a car accessories store? Show compatibility and mounting details next to the price, use in-car photos, and make sure the mobile layout works with one hand.

How should product pages differ for car accessories and home & kitchen products? Use the same layout but different spec fields. Car accessories need fit, mounting and power details. Home & kitchen products need dimensions, capacity, materials and cleaning instructions.

How big should buttons be on a mobile product page? WCAG 2.2 sets a minimum target size of 24 × 24 CSS pixels. For primary actions such as “Add to cart”, around 44 pixels is more comfortable.

Do FAQ sections still help SEO? Mostly indirectly. FAQ rich results are now limited, but clear on-page answers can match long-tail searches, reduce returns and keep shoppers on the page.

How can I reduce cart abandonment? Show total costs early, offer guest checkout, display delivery times and return terms before payment, and keep the checkout form short.

Good e-commerce UX is not about clever animations. It is about answering the shopper’s question before they have to ask it. Clear specs, honest photos, thumb-friendly controls and real FAQs turn an ordinary product page into one that helps people buy, whether they are shopping for car accessories or home & kitchen essentials.

#10606. Top 6 AI YouTube Transcript Generators: Features, Accuracy, and Limits

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A one-hour YouTube talk contains roughly nine thousand words, and finding the one point you need can take longer than watching the whole thing. A transcript solves that. With text in hand you can search for a phrase, quote an expert accurately, turn a webinar into a blog post, create subtitles or feed a summary tool.

Plenty of tools now promise instant YouTube transcripts, but they work in different ways. Some read the captions YouTube already has. Others listen to the audio themselves and produce a fresh transcript. That difference affects accuracy, speed and cost more than any feature list does. This guide compares six options, explains how each works and points out where each one falls short. Plans and limits change often, so confirm current details on each vendor's site.

Caption extraction. The tool pulls the captions attached to a video, either written by the creator or generated automatically by YouTube. It is fast and usually free, but it is only as good as those captions. YouTube notes that its automatic captions come from machine learning, so quality may vary, and accents, background noise and overlapping speakers can cause errors. Captions may also be missing entirely for some videos.

Fresh speech recognition. The tool takes the audio and runs its own speech-to-text model. This can be more accurate on difficult recordings and works when no captions exist, but it takes longer and often costs money.

Lynote is built for exactly this job. Paste a YouTube link, search by keyword or add a playlist, and it returns a timestamped transcript with an AI summary. Batch mode accepts up to 10 videos at once. The tool lists transcription in more than 100 languages, translation into 25, auto-chapters, one-click copy and TXT download, with SRT export also mentioned. The page says transcripts are free, with no sign-up required.

Best for: Students, researchers and marketers who want a quick, readable transcript or summary from a public video without installing anything.

Limitations: It extracts YouTube captions, so videos without captions cannot be processed. If the original captions are poor, the transcript will be too. For critical accuracy, review the text or use a service with human verification.

Before you install anything, check what YouTube already gives you. Many videos show a transcript option in the description area, and creators can review and edit automatic captions in YouTube Studio under Subtitles. YouTube offers automatic captions in dozens of languages for long-form videos and Shorts.

Best for: Quick lookups on videos that already have decent captions.

Limitations: Captions may not generate for videos with poor sound, long silences, overlapping speakers, mixed languages or unsupported languages. Export options are minimal, and there is no summary or cleanup.

Notta transcribes uploaded audio and video files and also accepts YouTube links. It lists support for 58 languages, real-time and bilingual transcription, and AI Notes that produce summaries and action items. A free plan includes 120 transcription minutes per month at the time of writing.

Best for: Professionals who handle a mix of recordings, meetings and YouTube videos in one place.

Limitations: The free minutes disappear quickly if you process long videos, and the strongest features sit behind paid plans.

Descript is an audio and video editor built around text. It transcribes recordings automatically, and deleting a word in the transcript removes the matching footage. You can import video, audio or a Zoom recording, and there is a free tier that does not require a credit card.

Best for: Creators who want to clean up their own videos and podcasts, not just read a transcript.

Limitations: It is an editor first, so it is more than you need for a quick YouTube lookup. It works with files you own, so you need the source media rather than a public link.

Rev offers both AI and human transcription. It advertises AI transcripts at 96 percent or better accuracy in minutes, and human transcripts at 99 percent or better, guaranteed within 12 hours. AI transcription covers 37 or more languages, while human transcription is English only. Pay-as-you-go rates start at $0.25 per minute for AI, and human transcription is listed at $1.99 per minute.

Best for: Legal, research and media work where mistakes are costly and a human check is worth paying for.

Limitations: The cost climbs for long videos, and the human option is slower than instant AI tools. Those accuracy figures are the vendor's own claims, so test your own audio.

Whisper is OpenAI's open-source speech recognition model, released under the MIT license. It handles multilingual transcription, translation into English and language identification. You install it with pip and need ffmpeg, and it comes in several model sizes from tiny to large, so you can trade speed against accuracy and hardware demands.

Best for: Developers building their own pipeline and teams with sensitive audio they prefer to keep on their own machines.

Limitations: You supply the audio, which means downloading it yourself and respecting the video owner's rights and YouTube's terms. Larger models need a capable GPU, and there is no built-in interface for non-technical users.

Start with the source. If the video already has good captions, a link-based tool is the fastest route. If the audio is difficult or the stakes are high, pick a tool that runs its own recognition or offers human review. If you are processing hundreds of videos or handling private recordings, look at Whisper or a paid platform with bulk features.

Whatever you choose, treat the transcript as a first draft. Check names, numbers and technical terms against the video before you publish or quote anything, and credit the original creator.

There is no single best transcript generator. Caption-based tools are quick and free, recognition-based tools are better when captions fall short, and human-verified services make sense when accuracy matters most. Try two or three on a video you know well, compare the results and keep a human in the loop for anything you publish.

Can I get a transcript if a video has no captions? Caption-extraction tools cannot. You need a tool that runs its own speech recognition on the audio, such as the options above that transcribe files.

Are AI transcripts accurate enough to publish? Often they are close, but not perfect. Accents, noise and technical terms cause mistakes, so review before publishing.

Is it legal to transcribe someone else's YouTube video? Transcribing for personal research or accessibility is common, but republishing the text may infringe copyright. Check the video's license and YouTube's terms, and get permission when in doubt.

What is the difference between a transcript and subtitles? A transcript is the full text, while subtitles are timed text shown on screen. Many tools can export both, often as SRT files.

#10602. Best Static Website Hosting Providers in 2027: Free and Paid Options Compared

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You've finished building your website. The HTML, CSS, and JavaScript files are ready, everything works locally, and now you need somewhere to publish it.

Static website hosting is one of the simplest ways to get a website online. Many providers offer free plans, and you don't need to maintain a traditional web server.

The harder part is choosing the right provider. Some let you upload a single HTML file and share it immediately. Others connect to GitHub, automatically deploy code changes, and support entire development teams.

We've compared eight static hosting providers, including TiinyHost, Cloudflare Pages, Netlify, Vercel, and GitHub Pages. Below, you'll find their free hosting limits, pricing, advantages, and drawbacks, along with recommendations for different types of projects.

Static website hosting is a service that stores and delivers pre-built website files, including HTML, CSS, JavaScript, images, and fonts.

Unlike traditional server-rendered websites, static sites don't need a server to generate each page when someone visits. The HTML is already prepared and can be delivered directly to the visitor's browser.

That doesn't mean static websites can't be interactive. JavaScript can still handle navigation, forms, filtering, and other features. Static sites can also communicate with external APIs or databases.

The main distinction is how pages are generated.

You can write a static website using plain HTML or generate one with frameworks and tools such as Astro, Hugo, Eleventy, or Next.js static export.

Static hosting is also commonly associated with Jamstack, an approach that separates the front end from back-end services.

Lower hosting costs: Many providers let you publish smaller websites for free.

Simpler maintenance: There's no application server or server-side runtime to maintain for the static pages themselves.

Efficient content delivery: Static files can be cached across a content delivery network (CDN), reducing the distance between visitors and website assets.

There are limitations, too. If your website requires user authentication, database operations, or server-side functionality, you'll need additional services or a hosting platform that supports them.

We compared eight providers based on their deployment methods, free plan allowances, pricing, and suitability for different projects.

Free hosting limits: Bandwidth, storage, build allowances, and restrictions.

Deployment process: Whether you can upload files directly or need a Git repository.

Custom domains and HTTPS: Support for publishing under your own domain.

Development features: Automatic deployments, preview environments, and integrations.

Pricing: What happens when a project exceeds its free allowance.

Usage restrictions: Whether free hosting is suitable for commercial projects.

The following table summarizes the main differences between all eight services.

Free plans aren't directly comparable. For example, Vercel restricts its Hobby plan to personal, non-commercial use, while GitHub Pages prohibits using the service as free hosting for an online business or commercial SaaS.

Bandwidth limits also aren't the same as visitor limits. A website serving large images or downloadable files can consume its monthly allowance with relatively few visitors.

Cloudflare Pages is a strong choice for developers who want to host static websites without worrying about a conventional monthly bandwidth cap.

You can connect a GitHub or GitLab repository, and Cloudflare automatically builds and deploys the website when you push changes. Direct uploads are also supported.

A maximum individual file size of 25 MiB.

The combination is useful for blogs, documentation websites, and other projects that primarily serve HTML, CSS, JavaScript, and images.

Cloudflare also provides preview deployments, custom domains, and HTTPS support.

No fixed bandwidth cap for static asset delivery.

Generous free plan for typical static websites.

Monthly build limits can matter for frequently updated projects.

Cloudflare's product range can make the initial setup less straightforward for beginners.

Cloudflare is increasingly emphasizing Workers Static Assets for new projects. Pages remains available, but developers starting a new application should compare both deployment approaches.

For a conventional static website, however, Pages is still a practical option, particularly when traffic could grow beyond the limits of other free plans.

See the official Cloudflare Pages limits for the current allowances.

Netlify combines static website hosting with tools designed to make development and deployment easier.

Connect a Git repository, configure your build settings, and Netlify can publish a new version whenever you push changes.

It also creates deploy previews, allowing you to inspect changes before merging them into your production branch.

This is useful when multiple people work on the same website or when you want to review design changes before publishing.

Netlify supports custom domains, HTTPS, serverless functions, and form handling.

Netlify's free plan uses a credit-based model with 300 credits per month.

Production deployments consume 15 credits each.

Web requests consume 2 credits per 10,000 requests.

Deploy previews and branch deployments consume no deployment credits.

The paid Personal plan starts at $9 per month and includes 1,000 monthly credits.

Convenient Git integration and deploy previews.

Built-in features such as forms and serverless functions.

Suitable for both simple websites and more advanced front-end projects.

Credit-based pricing requires monitoring several types of usage.

A busy site with frequent deployments can consume its free allowance faster than expected.

When comparing Netlify alternatives, Cloudflare Pages is particularly worth considering if your main concern is bandwidth.

For developers choosing between Vercel vs Netlify, the deciding factor is often the framework and workflow. Netlify offers a flexible approach to static site deployment, while Vercel has particularly close integration with Next.js.

Vercel is particularly well suited to Next.js applications, although it also supports React, Vue, Svelte, Astro, and other frameworks.

Like Netlify, Vercel connects to your Git repository and deploys updates automatically.

Every deployment receives its own URL, which helps developers review changes before publishing them to the main website.

Vercel also supports server-side rendering, functions, and other features beyond traditional static site hosting.

There is one important restriction: Vercel Hobby is intended for personal, non-commercial use only.

If you're deploying a business website or another commercial project, you'll need an appropriate paid plan.

Convenient deployment workflow for Next.js.

Supports both static and server-rendered applications.

Paid usage can involve multiple resource-based charges.

If you're building a personal Next.js application, Vercel is an obvious candidate. For a basic HTML website, however, its additional framework features may be unnecessary.

GitHub Pages is a straightforward way to publish a website directly from a GitHub repository.

It works particularly well for developer portfolios, project documentation, open-source websites, and small informational sites.

You can upload HTML, CSS, and JavaScript files to a repository, configure GitHub Pages, and publish them under a github.io subdomain.

GitHub Pages can serve existing static files or build websites using supported workflows, including Jekyll and GitHub Actions.

Published websites have a maximum size of 1 GB.

Bandwidth has a soft limit of 100 GB per month.

Free accounts can publish Pages sites from public repositories.

The bandwidth and build restrictions are soft limits, meaning they aren't necessarily enforced as strict cutoffs.

Simple integration with GitHub repositories.

Well suited to documentation and open-source projects.

Free accounts require a public repository for Pages.

GitHub Pages is not intended for running online businesses, e-commerce websites, or commercial SaaS products.

GitHub Pages is particularly convenient if your code already lives on GitHub and you only need to publish documentation or a portfolio.

For commercial websites, Cloudflare Pages or Netlify may be more appropriate.

The GitHub Pages documentation explains its hosting limits and usage restrictions.

Not every website needs a repository, deployment pipeline, or command-line interface.

If you've created a simple HTML page and want to share it, TiinyHost lets you upload an HTML file or ZIP archive and publish it under a shareable URL.

It can also host PDFs, making it useful for sharing documents and presentations alongside website projects.

The main advantage is the upload process. You don't need to configure a repository or install development tools before publishing.

Sharing an HTML file with someone who doesn't use Git.

The free plan includes one active site, a 3 MB project upload limit, and an allowance of 5,000 monthly visits.

The Tiny plan costs €5 per month when billed monthly. It supports uploads up to 25 MB and includes built-in analytics.

The Solo plan starts at €13 per month when billed monthly and adds features such as custom domains, password protection, and additional projects.

Annual billing is available at lower effective monthly prices.

No Git repository or command line required.

Suitable for previews and smaller projects.

The free plan's 3 MB limit is restrictive for websites with many images or other assets.

Custom domains require the Solo plan or higher.

Upload-based publishing is less convenient than Git automation for frequently updated websites.

For someone who simply needs free HTML hosting to share a small project, TiinyHost is worth considering.

For a larger production website that receives regular updates, Git-based hosting is likely to be more manageable.

Firebase Hosting is Google's hosting service for static websites and web application assets.

It integrates with other Firebase products, including Authentication, Cloud Firestore, and Cloud Functions.

That makes it useful when a website needs static hosting but also relies on Google's back-end services.

Deployment is handled through the Firebase command-line interface.

After configuring your project, you can deploy using:

Firebase distributes website assets through its CDN and supports custom domains with SSL certificates.

The Blaze plan uses pay-as-you-go billing while retaining applicable no-cost allowances.

One detail worth checking is the distinction between Firebase Hosting and Firebase App Hosting. They are separate products with different features and pricing models.

Integrates with Firebase Authentication and Firestore.

Useful for applications already using Google's developer services.

Initial setup is more involved than uploading an HTML file.

The daily transfer allowance may become restrictive for larger websites.

Firebase Hosting makes the most sense when Firebase is already part of your application.

For a standalone static blog or portfolio that doesn't use Firebase services, Cloudflare Pages or GitHub Pages may be simpler.

Amazon S3 and CloudFront offer a different approach to static hosting.

Instead of using a platform that handles the entire deployment process, you combine AWS services to store and deliver your website.

S3 stores the website files, while CloudFront distributes them through a CDN.

This setup provides control over caching, permissions, security, and integration with other AWS services.

It can be suitable for organizations already using AWS or developers who need specific infrastructure configurations.

AWS uses usage-based pricing for S3 storage, requests, and applicable CloudFront usage.

Free allowances may apply depending on your account and the current AWS pricing program, but you should calculate costs based on your expected traffic, storage, and requests.

Unlike simpler hosting services, there isn't one fixed monthly price for every website.

Several services can contribute to the final bill.

Incorrect permissions or origin settings can cause access errors.

One common issue when setting up S3 static website hosting is receiving a 403 Forbidden error because of bucket permissions or CloudFront origin configuration.

AWS recommends using CloudFront Origin Access Control with a regular S3 bucket origin so that the bucket can remain private. This differs from exposing the S3 website endpoint directly, which does not support HTTPS on its own.

AWS S3 and CloudFront are worth considering if you need that level of control. For a small static website, a managed hosting platform usually requires less setup.

Render is a cloud platform that supports static websites alongside web services, databases, background workers, and other application components.

Its static hosting works through a Git repository.

Connect your repository, select the build command and publish directory, and Render automatically deploys your website.

The service includes CDN delivery, custom domains, and managed TLS certificates.

Render is particularly useful when your project includes both a static front end and back-end services that you want to manage through the same platform.

Render's Hobby workspace is free and includes:

500 standard build pipeline minutes per month.

Additional bandwidth is billed at $0.15 per GB under the current pricing structure, so the Hobby workspace should not be treated as unlimited free hosting.

The Pro workspace costs $25 per month, excluding any applicable usage-based compute charges.

Static websites and back-end services on one platform.

The 5 GB included bandwidth allowance is relatively small.

Render makes sense when you're already using it for other parts of an application.

If you only need to host a static HTML website, Cloudflare Pages offers a more generous bandwidth allowance without the additional platform complexity.

Free static website hosting can be sufficient for a personal portfolio, documentation site, prototype, or small informational website.

However, the restrictions vary considerably between providers.

Some hosts measure data transfer, while others use visitor counts or a credit system. Check how each provider calculates usage rather than comparing headline numbers alone.

If your website rebuilds whenever you publish content, deployment limits matter. A blog updated a few times a month has different requirements from an application receiving dozens of deployments every day.

Free hosting doesn't always mean you can run a business website. Vercel's Hobby plan and GitHub Pages have restrictions that may rule out certain commercial projects.

Some services include custom domains on free plans. Others require an upgrade before you can connect one.

A free plan might stop serving additional usage, while another provider may bill for overages.

Before publishing a production website, check the provider's billing behavior and configure spending controls where available.

The right service depends primarily on how you build your website and what you intend to do with it.

For most developers publishing a conventional static website, Cloudflare Pages is a reasonable starting point.

If you're working with Git and want convenient previews and deployment tools, Netlify is another good choice.

For small files, temporary demos, and client previews, a simpler upload-based service may save time.

You should also consider how often the website will change.

Uploading files manually may be fine for a page that rarely changes. For a website with regular content updates, automated deployments from Git will generally be easier to maintain.

Publishing your website is only the first step. If you want search engines to discover and understand your pages, check the technical SEO basics before sharing the URL.

Every important page should have a descriptive HTML title and a relevant meta description.

You can use the Meta Tags Generator to create the necessary markup.

Open Graph tags help control how a page appears when shared on platforms that support them.

Set the title, description, canonical page URL where appropriate, and preview image.

A robots.txt file tells compliant crawlers which paths they may access. It doesn't guarantee indexing or prevent a public page from appearing in search results.

Use the Robots.txt Generator to create your crawler rules.

Include a sitemap with the canonical URLs you want search engines to discover.

Structured data helps search engines interpret eligible content.

Depending on the website, you may want Organization, Article, BreadcrumbList, or other relevant schema types.

The JSON-LD Schema Generator can help generate markup.

Confirm the correct domain loads over HTTPS.

Check canonical tags and redirect behavior.

Make sure important pages don't contain unintended noindex directives.

Test page performance and Core Web Vitals.

Verify the website in Google Search Console.

Static hosting makes serving website files relatively straightforward, but technical SEO still requires configuration and testing.

Cloudflare Pages is a good general-purpose choice because of its free static asset bandwidth and Git integration. Netlify is well suited to teams that need deploy previews, while GitHub Pages works well for portfolios and documentation. The best option depends on your deployment workflow, traffic requirements, and whether the website is commercial.

Yes. TiinyHost, Cloudflare Pages, Netlify, GitHub Pages, Firebase Hosting, Render, and several other services offer free hosting options. Most free plans have restrictions involving bandwidth, deployments, storage, or usage. Check the terms before using a free plan for a business website.

A small static website can cost nothing to host if it stays within a provider's free allowances. Paid plans vary from fixed monthly subscriptions to usage-based pricing. Domain registration, paid back-end services, and additional storage or bandwidth may increase the total cost.

For a standalone HTML file, upload-based hosting is generally the simplest approach. Services such as TiinyHost let you publish a file without configuring Git or a build process. If the project already lives in a GitHub repository, GitHub Pages or Cloudflare Pages may be more convenient.

GitHub Pages is a sensible option for documentation, open-source projects, and personal portfolios. Netlify offers more deployment features, including preview environments, forms, and functions. It also supports commercial projects, making it more appropriate for some business websites that would fall outside GitHub Pages' permitted uses.

Static website hosting doesn't need to be expensive or complicated. The important part is choosing a service that matches your website and deployment process.

Cloudflare Pages is a good starting point for static websites that need generous free bandwidth. Netlify is useful for Git-based development, while GitHub Pages suits personal projects and documentation.

If you're publishing a small HTML file or sharing a prototype, upload-based hosting may be all you need.

#10597. A Practical Introduction to the Twitch Helix API

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Behind almost every Twitch tool you have ever seen, the stat trackers, the moderation bots, the stream overlays, the alert systems, sits one interface: the Twitch Helix API. Twitch is unusually generous with public data, and Helix is the door to it. If you have ever wondered how third-party sites know who is live, what a channel's viewership looks like, or details a Twitch profile page does not even display, this is the machinery.

This article is a practical overview of Helix for developers who have not used it yet: what it offers, how its authentication works, what people build with it, and where the sharp edges are.

Helix is Twitch's current REST API, served from api.twitch.tv/helix. It replaced the old "Kraken" API, which was fully retired in 2022, so any tutorial still showing Kraken endpoints is a sign to keep scrolling. Helix covers users, streams, channels, games and categories, clips, chat, moderation actions, subscriptions, and events, with JSON in and JSON out.

Two things distinguish it from many platform APIs. First, a large share of it works without any user's permission, because the data is public by Twitch's own definition. Second, it is paired with EventSub, a push system that notifies your application when things happen (a stream goes live, a user follows, a message arrives) instead of forcing you to poll. Most serious Twitch tools use both: Helix for lookups, EventSub for live events.

Authentication is where most newcomers stall, mainly because Twitch offers several OAuth flows and tutorials rarely say which one you actually need. The short version:

Every request needs a Client ID, which you get by registering an application in the Twitch developer console (two-factor authentication on your account is required first). Requests carry the Client ID and a bearer token together; forgetting the Client ID header is the classic mystery 401.

App access tokens come from the client credentials flow, a single server-to-server request with your ID and secret. They have no user attached and that is fine, because public reads, user profiles, live streams, top games, clips, need no user context. For most read-only tools, this is the only flow you ever touch.

Tokens expire, so real applications cache them with their reported lifetime and refresh on a 401 rather than assuming a token lives forever.

A tour of the endpoints that power most tools:

Users. Give the users endpoint a username and it returns the account's public record: display name, profile image, description, Affiliate or Partner status, and the account's exact creation timestamp. That last field is interesting because Twitch does not display it anywhere on a profile, yet it is one of the platform's most useful trust signals, moderators use account age to spot throwaway accounts, and streamers use it to filter giveaway entries. The creation date alone powers a small subcategory of tools: free account age checkers, such as Twitch account age checker, are essentially this one endpoint with a clean page around it, taking any public username and returning the creation date, age, avatar, and broadcaster status. It is a good illustration of how far a single well-chosen Helix field can go.

Streams. The stream's endpoint reports who is live right now, with titles, categories, thumbnail URLs, and viewer counts. This is the backbone of every "is my favorite streamer live" notifier and every directory-style site, and polling it over time is how stat trackers chart viewership history.

Games and categories. Top games, category search, and metadata. Combined with streams data, this is how analytics sites rank categories and how tools answer "how crowded is this category right now."

Clips and videos. Clip metadata, view counts, and creation tools. Highlight compilers and clip-sharing bots live here.

Helix enforces a points-based rate limit per Client ID, and every response tells you where you stand through Ratelimit-Limit, Ratelimit-Remaining, and Ratelimit-Reset headers. Well-behaved tools read those headers and back off before hitting zero; badly behaved ones retry 429s in a loop and wonder why things get worse. For lookup-style tools the practical fix is caching, data like account creation dates never changes, so there is no reason to ask twice.

A few more edges worth knowing before they bite:

Usernames change, IDs do not. Store user IDs, not logins, or your saved data will eventually point at the wrong person.

Missing users are not errors. Looking up a nonexistent username returns a successful response with an empty data array, so handle the empty case explicitly.

Batching exists. The users endpoint takes up to 100 logins in one call, which matters the moment your tool checks lists instead of individuals.

Secrets stay server-side. The client secret identifies your application; calling the token endpoint from browser code hands your app's identity to anyone who opens devtools. Public-facing tools put a small server between the browser and Twitch.

The reason Twitch's third-party ecosystem is so large is that the barrier is so low: one public API, generous free access, and a platform where small questions genuinely matter to people. That is why the space is full of narrow tools that each answer one thing well, when was this account created, is this channel live, how fast is this category growing, which clips popped this week. Most of them are a weekend of work on top of one or two endpoints, and some of them grow into real products.

If you want to start, the path is short: register an application at the developer console, request an app access token, and call the users or streams endpoint for something you actually care about. The official documentation at dev.twitch.tv is current and well maintained, which is not something every platform can claim. Pick one piece of data Twitch collects but does not display, put a clean interface on it, and you are following the same recipe as most of the Twitch tools you already use.

#10592. Self-Hosted LangSmith Alternatives: 4 Tools Tested on One TypeScript Agent

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

LangSmith is closed-source and restricts self-hosting to enterprise licenses, teams that run their own LLMOps stack and want to keep traces on their own servers need an alternative. To find the best fit, we set a baseline by tracing a small TypeScript agent (equipped with two tools) in LangSmith Cloud. Then, we ran the exact same agent through Langfuse, Arize Phoenix, Opik, and AcruxCore using each platform's recommended TypeScript setup. All tests were run on November 5, 2026, and we pulled every trace back through each tool's API to verify the data.

All four alternatives successfully recreated LangSmith’s trace tree for the test prompt: a single agent span containing three LLM calls and two tool calls. The real differences between them come down to how much boilerplate code you have to write, how spans are typed, whether costs are tracked, and the overall server footprint.

Why Teams Look for a LangSmith Alternative

LangSmith remains the best choice if you already use LangChain and allow cloud hosting—it required just 25 lines of code in our test. The alternatives below are for teams that don't fit that profile.

To test these platforms, we built a simple customer support agent equipped with two tools:

Both tools use static fixture data to ensure every test run is identical. The agent runs a standard loop: it prompts the model, executes any requested tools, and stops once the model provides a final answer.

We tested the agent with three specific prompts to see how the tracing handled different levels of complexity:

For our comparison, we focused entirely on the third question because it generates a robust, multi-step trace.

Under the hood, the agent uses the standard openai package to call the qwen3.8-flash model. We originally picked this model based on its reliable tool-calling behavior during early probing thought it once skipped a tool call later.

We used LangSmith as our baseline. By tracing the agent there first, we created a checklist of every field it captured, and we judged all the alternatives against that exact list.

For setup, we followed the recommended TypeScript approach from each tool's documentation. Phoenix didn't have specific instructions for using its register() helper with OpenAI in TypeScript, so we adapted the pattern from their Anthropic guide.

Here is the agent with no tracing. Each tool section adds one tracing file that imports these files and changes nothing in them.

LangSmith traces the plain openai package with two helpers. wrapOpenAI records each model call, and traceable records your own functions. Without traceable on the run and the tools, wrapOpenAI alone gave three separate root runs and no tool runs.

This file adds 25 lines and removes 3, and installs one package.

LangSmith showed one root run with five children in call order: LLM, get_order_status, LLM, get_refund_policy, LLM. Every field it recorded became a row of the checklist table under "Results". Cost appeared only after a custom price entry. In our error run the failing tool was marked as an error, but the root stayed success: traceable turned the throw into a rejected promise that our agent did not await, so the agent carried on and answered.

Langfuse self-hosts as six containers, including ClickHouse, a column store built for large scans. In our measurement the stack held 2.60–2.64 GiB of memory at idle. Its Docker Compose guide recommends at least 4 CPU cores and 16 GiB RAM, with about 100 GiB of disk.

The TypeScript path is built on OpenTelemetry. You start the OTel Node SDK with LangfuseSpanProcessor, wrap the client with observeOpenAI, and wrap your own functions with observe:

This file adds 31 lines and removes 5, and installs four packages. We pinned the OTel Node SDK to 0.223.0, because @langfuse/otel 6.1.1 does not accept 0.300.0.

Langfuse successfully recreated the same trace tree, neatly tagging the spans as AGENT, GENERATION, and TOOL. It captured all the essentials: tool calls, tool definitions, and the complete message history. However, for the LLM output, it only saved the assistant's message—dropping the finish_reason, response ID, and the provider name.

We ran our deployment in Langfuse v4's events_only mode, which turns off the v1 trace endpoint. Because of this, the API didn't return a centralized trace object or root-level sums for tokens and costs, even though the UI calculated and displayed them perfectly (we didn't test other modes).

Costs showed up exactly as expected once we added a custom price entry. Since Langfuse calculates prices based on the specific usage types you define, and we only set rates for input and output, the separate reasoning tokens went unpriced. Finally, during our error test, the observe wrapper let the error bubble up to the agent, correctly flagging both the tool and the root span as an ERROR.

Phoenix self-hosts as two containers, one Python server and Postgres. In our measurement it held 0.47 GiB at idle. Phoenix is source-available under the Elastic License 2.0, so it is not open source. Authentication is off until you turn it on.

The TypeScript path uses Phoenix's register() helper and the OpenInference instrumentation for the OpenAI package. OpenInference is a set of OpenTelemetry attribute names for LLM spans. traceAgent and traceTool wrap the run and the tools:

This file adds 32 lines and removes 3, and installs three packages.

Phoenix rebuilt the same tree, typed as AGENT, LLM and TOOL. Each LLM span keeps the full OpenAI response object, plus the messages as flattened attributes. The root span stores no token count, but Phoenix computes a sum for the root and the trace when you read it. Cost and those sums are only in the GraphQL API and the UI, and the REST API has no per-trace endpoint.

Cost appeared after a custom price entry under Settings > Models, and Phoenix priced the reasoning tokens by falling back to the output price. In the error run, the tool span and the root were marked ERROR, and the tool span carried an exception event with the stack trace.

Opik self-hosts as eight containers, including ClickHouse, MySQL and ZooKeeper. In our measurement it held 3.69 GiB at idle, the most of the four. The self-hosted UI opened without a login, so every trace was readable from any machine that could reach the port.

The TypeScript path wraps the client with trackOpenAI and your own functions with track. By default trackOpenAI starts a new trace for every model call, so the file passes the run's span through the documented parent option:

This file adds 33 lines and removes 5. It installs opik-openai 2.2.142, and npm adds opik 1.11.14 as its peer, because opik-openai asks for opik ^1.8.61.

Opik successfully recreated the trace tree, and its trace object neatly tallies token counts across all LLM spans. In the logs, tool arguments are bundled into an array, and the LLM output only retains the assistant's message.

We did notice a quirk with the documented OpenAI wrapper (tested on the npm package opik 1.11.14): it tags model calls as general rather than llm, which incorrectly leaves the llm_span_count at zero. (We didn't test newer Opik core versions to see if this has changed). Additionally, because our synchronous tools execute in under a millisecond, they log the exact same start time as the subsequent LLM call, causing the UI to list the LLM call first out of order.

Cost tracking is entirely manual. Opik showed no costs by default, and the documentation doesn't mention a custom price table. We checked the configuration tabs on our 2.2.52 instance (Feedback definitions, AI Providers, Workspace preferences) and found no model pricing settings. To see costs, you have to manually assign a price to each individual span yourself—only the spans we hardcoded this way displayed a cost.

Finally, error reporting is highly detailed. If something fails, Opik attaches an error_info block—complete with the error type, message, and full traceback—directly to the tool span, the root span, and the overall trace.

AcruxCore self-hosts as five containers: Postgres, Redis, a Node API and worker, and nginx. In our measurement it held 0.39 GiB at idle, the least of the four. Its quickstart leaves sign-up open and ships a committed default GATEWAY_ENCRYPTION_KEY, which encrypts stored provider keys; replace it. We tested a local build from 2026-09-17.

The TypeScript path AcruxCore's docs put first is its gateway. The official openai client keeps working: you point its baseURL at ACRUXCORE_BASE_URL + "/gateway" and call a model registered in AcruxCore, which forwards each call to an OpenAI-compatible provider. The gateway records every model call as an llm span. The SDK's step helpers add the rest: traces.step opens the agent span, traces.wrap wraps each tool, and two headers file the gateway's spans under the agent span:

This file adds 30 lines and removes 5, and installs one package. It builds its own client, so it does not use provider.ts. The gateway refused our team API key, so the client uses a gateway virtual key created in the dashboard.

On this path every model call passes through the AcruxCore server, which none of the other four tools need. If you do not want a proxy in the request path, the SDK's register() helper sends OpenTelemetry spans from your process instead. On that path the step helpers do not join the openai package's spans, so you write the agent and tool spans by hand.

AcruxCore rebuilt the same tree, typed as agent, llm and tool, in call order. Each LLM span keeps the full response, the provider and the cached-token count, and the trace carries a token total. The LLM spans do not keep the tool definitions sent to the model, and no reasoning-token count was recorded. The trace took its name from the question, not from the agent span, and each LLM span is named by its start time.

Cost appeared on every LLM span and as a sum on the trace, from the custom price we set on the model in AcruxCore's model list. In the error run, the step helpers marked the tool span and the agent root as error with the error message, with no status code of ours. The API returned no stack trace.

All five tools accept traces over OTLP, the OpenTelemetry wire format. trace-otel.ts sends the same spans with no vendor package: the OTel Node SDK, the OTLP/HTTP protobuf exporter and the OpenInference OpenAI instrumentation. The exporter reads its endpoint and headers from environment variables:

We ran the two-tool question once per backend and changed only environment variables: the endpoint, the headers, and for Phoenix a resource attribute that sets the project name (without it, spans go to a default project). "Accepted" means the exporter reported success, which it does only for an HTTP 2xx, and we read the spans back through each API.

Opik stored the root and tool spans as general although AGENT and TOOL kinds were sent; the cause is Not tested. LangSmith mapped AGENT to its chain type. If you instrument with plain OTel and OpenInference attributes, your raw spans are not tied to one vendor, but span types, cost and trace views still differ by backend.

The first table covers setup: how much server each tool needs, how much code we changed in the agent, and how cost and the plain OTel exporter worked. Phoenix and AcruxCore had the smallest servers, and AcruxCore needed the fewest lines among the self-hosted tools. Opik was the only one without a price table.

The second table checks each trace against what LangSmith recorded for the same run, one field at a time. All five rebuilt the same tree with tokens and durations. They differ in span types, how much of the LLM output they keep, where token sums live, and how much detail they store for the failing tool.

Token totals for the two-tool question ranged from 1,910 to 2,111. Each tool traced a separate run and reasoning length varies, so these numbers do not compare the tools.

All five tools successfully traced our test agent, so the right choice really comes down to your team's specific constraints, infrastructure preferences, and how tracing fits into the rest of your MLOps tooling.

All four alternatives successfully recreated LangSmith’s trace tree. The real deciding factors are boilerplate code, span typing, cost tracking, and server bloat.

LangSmith remains the simplest option for LangChain users who can utilize the cloud. Otherwise, your choice depends on your priorities: Langfuse for community size, Phoenix for a lightweight Postgres footprint, Opik for strict Apache-2.0 compliance with deep error logging, or AcruxCore for combined tracing and tool governance on minimal hardware. Best of all, if you stick to standard OpenTelemetry spans, you aren't locked in—you can try them all and easily pivot later.

#10586. How to Build an Accessible Customer Reviews Section with HTML and CSS

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Customer reviews are often displayed on business websites, but presenting them effectively requires more than adding a few star icons and customer quotes. Developers need to consider semantic HTML, responsive layouts, accessibility, and how review content fits into the overall user experience.

A well-built review section makes customer feedback easy to read without distracting visitors from the rest of the page. It should also work across different screen sizes and remain understandable to people using assistive technologies.

Let's look at how to build one using standard HTML and CSS.

The first step is to structure review content properly. Instead of placing everything inside generic

elements, use meaningful HTML elements to describe the content.

For example, a review can use

as its container and
for the customer's actual feedback.

This structure separates the review from its attribution and gives the section a clear heading. The rating also includes an accessible label so screen-reader users can understand what the stars represent.

For real websites, developers should display genuine customer feedback and avoid presenting invented testimonials as actual customer experiences.

A review section might look attractive on a desktop but become difficult to read on smaller screens. CSS Grid provides a straightforward way to create a flexible layout without relying on JavaScript.

The auto-fit and minmax() combination allows cards to adjust to the available space. Instead of defining a fixed number of columns for every device, the browser fits as many cards as the container can comfortably accommodate.

Developers working with more complex interfaces can explore SitePoint's guide to responsive web design for additional layout techniques.

A responsive layout should remain readable when viewed on a phone. Avoid fixed card widths, excessive padding, and text sizes that force users to zoom.

You can also adjust spacing at smaller viewport widths:

Test the section at several viewport sizes rather than relying on one desktop preview. Check that long customer names wrap correctly, cards do not overflow, and text remains comfortable to read.

Some review components use animated sliders, automatic scrolling, or hover effects. These features can create problems for visitors who prefer reduced motion or navigate with a keyboard.

A simple approach is to avoid unnecessary animation. If transitions are included, respect the visitor's system preference:

The MDN guide to using media queries for accessibility explains how developers can adapt interfaces to user preferences.

Also make sure interactive controls have visible keyboard focus styles. A carousel should provide usable previous and next controls, clear labels, and a way to pause automatic movement if it is used.

On a production website, reviews may come from a database, a content management system, or an external review service. Keeping the content separate from the presentation makes the component easier to maintain.

Businesses can also use an online reputation management platform to monitor customer feedback and understand how their brand is perceived across online channels.

For example, a developer could store each review with fields such as:

The frontend can then render the same component for every review. This avoids manually creating separate HTML for each customer and makes future design changes easier.

Developers should also validate incoming data, handle missing fields, and avoid displaying private customer information without permission.

Before publishing, check the component in different browsers and screen sizes. Test keyboard navigation, screen-reader labels, text wrapping, and color contrast. If reviews are loaded dynamically, check how the section behaves when there are no reviews or when the data request fails.

A useful review component does not need complicated animations or a large JavaScript library. Semantic HTML, flexible CSS Grid layouts, accessible labels, and reliable content handling provide a strong foundation.

Customer feedback can only be useful on a website when visitors can understand and navigate it easily. By treating reviews as a proper interface component rather than decorative content, developers can create sections that are maintainable, accessible, and consistent with the rest of the website.

#10580. Build a Hardware Diagnostic Log with Vanilla JavaScript

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A device disconnects halfway through a test. The application reports a timeout, the firmware restarts, and the next attempt succeeds. Where should you start?

For developers working with connected devices, browser interfaces, or hardware prototypes, an application error is often only the final symptom. Replacing code without recording the conditions around a failure can leave you chasing the wrong layer.

This tutorial builds a small browser-based diagnostic log. It records measurements, preserves the test conditions, and exports evidence for another developer to review. You need basic HTML and JavaScript knowledge; no framework or connected instrument is required.

1. Define the question before collecting data Consider a hypothetical development board that disconnects when a peripheral starts. Several explanations remain possible: a firmware exception, a transport problem, or a power supply issue. Start with a testable question: “Does the measured supply voltage change when this peripheral starts?” That question tells you what to record and what to keep constant.

Use a known-good board of the same revision where available. Match the firmware, cable, supply, peripheral, and workload. A comparison loses value when several conditions change together.

Record the observation separately from the explanation:

The hypothesis remains provisional. A reading taken after the device restarts does not establish what happened during the failure.

2. Choose evidence that answers the question Application logs, firmware traces, and bench measurements answer different questions. A timeout establishes that an operation did not finish in time; it does not identify a failed component.

For a view of specialist equipment used in phone-board troubleshooting, DIYFIXTOOL’s overview of Chip-Level Diagnostic Tools discusses thermal imaging, BGA X-ray inspection, and inductance testing. Treat it as a vendor-authored introduction to equipment categories; use instrument documentation and board-specific references when interpreting measurements.

The log below deliberately leaves interpretation to the operator. Enter a numeric value, its unit, and the conditions under which it was measured. Do not turn an isolated reading into an automatic “faulty chip” verdict.

For physical measurements, follow the board and instrument documentation and work within your training. This tutorial covers the software record rather than a procedure for probing or repairing a powered device. 3. Build a small, accessible form

Save the following as index.html and serve it through your usual local development server. For example, if Python is installed, run python -m http. server 8000 in its directory, then open http://localhost:8000. 4. Preserve data without hiding failures

The application stores an array of records as JSON. On reload, it parses that JSON and checks the expected field types before rendering it. Parsing alone does not validate the structure of stored data.

SitePoint’s guide to looping through JSON responses provides useful background on working with objects and arrays after parsing JSON. Each table cell uses textContent, so a note containing HTML is displayed as text rather than inserted as markup. The MDN documentation for textContent explains this behavior.

Storage operations sit inside try...catch blocks because browser persistence can fail. The interface keeps new measurements in memory and offers an export when saving fails. It does not claim those measurements will survive a reload. For another practical vanilla JavaScript project, SitePoint’s **Markdown previewer tutorial **explores browser UI construction and persistence.

As **MDN’s localStorage documentation **explains, storage belongs to the document’s origin. Opening the application on a different port may therefore show a different log. This prototype is a single-browser tool, so export important sessions and avoid recording customer identifiers or private device contents.

5. Compare measurements under matching conditions Suppose two hypothetical readings are 3.29 V and 3.08 V. Their difference is −0.21 V. That arithmetic is straightforward; its meaning depends on the rail specification, measurement uncertainty, workload, and timing.

Do not add a universal voltage threshold to the application. A device-specific limit should come from its specification or an established test procedure.

If the failure is brief, a manually entered steady-state measurement may miss it entirely. Record that limitation. The next investigation may require a time-correlated capture rather than another static reading.

When sharing a session, include the firmware revision, board revision, instrument settings, and reproduction steps. A colleague should be able to repeat the comparison without guessing how the reading was obtained. 6. Verify the workflow before relying on it Try adding a decimal reading and a negative reading, then reload the page. Confirm that both values and their units remain intact. Enter a note containing test and check that it appears literally in the table.

Export the JSON and inspect the record count. Then simulate blocked storage and confirm that the interface reports the failure while allowing an export of the current session.

For a larger application, add editable entries, instrument metadata, and explicit baseline records. A shared service would also need access controls, server-side validation, and a policy for retention. These additions should follow the needs of the diagnostic workflow.

Turn a symptom into a reproducible investigation

The finished prototype captures a useful minimum: what was measured, where, when, and under which conditions. Its value comes from preserving evidence that another person can inspect.

When an application error crosses into firmware or hardware, that record helps you choose the next experiment. Repeat the failure, change one variable, and document what the new evidence supports before deciding what to fix.

#10576. Google Workspace vs. Microsoft 365 vs. Proton Workspace: Which Is Best for Developers and Freelancers?

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Think about what's sitting in your inbox and Drive right now. Client contracts and invoices. Project specs under NDA. Staging credentials a client emailed you. That API key or password you meant to delete.

For most developers and freelancers, all of it lives in Google Workspace or Microsoft 365. Both are excellent tools, and both encrypt your data in transit and at rest. But in their standard setup, the provider holds the encryption keys, which means your provider (and the integrated apps and AI features connected to it) can access what's inside.

Proton Workspace takes a different approach: the same everyday tools, with encryption designed so that not even Proton can read your data.

Here's how the three compare, what each does best, and which makes the most sense for how you work.

Google and Microsoft are built around ecosystem integration. Proton is built around keeping your data yours, without sacrificing productivity tools.

Google Workspace is the go-to suite for startups, agencies and freelancers, and for good reason. Gmail, Drive, Docs, Sheets, Calendar and Meet work together seamlessly, and several people can edit the same file in real time without emailing versions back and forth.

Google encrypts data at rest and in transit, and offers client-side encryption on eligible editions. But that extra layer requires higher-tier plans and configuration, so in the default experience Google holds the keys. A password manager and VPN aren't part of the suite.

Best for: Teams whose top priorities are real-time collaboration and third-party integrations.

If your work runs on Word, Excel and Outlook, Microsoft 365 needs little introduction. Business Standard includes full desktop apps, and Excel remains the benchmark for heavy spreadsheet work.

That breadth comes with complexity. Licensing, permissions and device management can eat into time a small team or solo developer would rather spend shipping. And like Google, Microsoft's everyday productivity experience isn't built around end-to-end encryption, while Copilot and advanced security features add cost.

Best for: Businesses built on desktop Office apps and established Microsoft workflows.

Everything is managed from one admin console, with one bill.

This is the core difference. Proton uses zero-access encryption: your data is encrypted before it reaches Proton's servers, and Proton never holds the keys. Proton can't read your data. Not won't. Can't.

The honest trade-offs: Proton Docs and Sheets cover core editing and collaboration, not every advanced Excel feature. There's no presentation app yet, and the third-party integration ecosystem is smaller than Google's or Microsoft's.

US prices for comparable plans with annual billing, excluding taxes:

The headline prices are close. The real cost isn't:

Proton Workspace covers business email, 1 TB of encrypted storage, docs, sheets, video calls, a password manager and a VPN. That's one bill instead of three.

Need more? Workspace Premium and adds 3 TB of storage per user, 20 custom domains, meetings for up to 250 participants, Lumo private AI and email data retention policies for only $7 more per month. Both plans include a 14-day free trial, and annual plans are currently up to 20% off.

Choose Google Workspace if your team lives in real-time collaboration and relies heavily on Google's integration ecosystem.

Choose Microsoft 365 if your work depends on desktop Excel, PowerPoint and established Microsoft workflows.

Less than you might expect. Proton's Easy Switch tool imports email, contacts and calendars from Google Workspace and other providers, and you keep your custom domain, so clients won't notice a thing. Smaller teams can migrate on their own, and Proton's business team supports larger moves.

Moving from Microsoft 365? Check the migration options for each data type rather than assuming every workflow transfers automatically.

Is Proton Workspace a good alternative to Google Workspace for developers?

Can Google Workspace or Microsoft 365 read my email and files?

By default, yes. Google Workspace and Microsoft 365 hold the encryption keys, and their default terms give them access to your data.

No. Zero-access encryption means Proton never holds the keys to your stored data. Emails between Proton users are end-to-end encrypted automatically, and you can send password-protected encrypted emails to anyone.

Yes. Proton's apps are open source and independently audited.

Can I use Proton Mail with my existing email client?

Yes. Proton Mail Bridge connects Outlook, Apple Mail and Thunderbird.

Does Google Workspace or Microsoft 365 use my data to train AI?

It can. If your plan allows Google Workspace or Microsoft 365 to hold the encryption keys, they can access your data and use it to train their integrated AI models.

No. Proton can't read your data, so it can't use it for AI training.

Google Workspace and Microsoft 365 are mature, capable platforms, and if your work depends on advanced Excel or a deep web of Google integrations, there's a good case for using them.

But if you're a freelancer or a developer or running a small team, Proton Workspace is the stronger choice. You get the same core tools for less money, a password manager and VPN you'd otherwise pay for separately, and encryption that keeps client data, code and credentials readable only by you.

You don't have to take that on faith, either. The code is open source, and the trial is free.

#10571. Prompt Management in TypeScript: LangSmith vs Git vs 3 Open-Source Registries

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every prompt registry we tested can change a prompt in a running TypeScript app, roll it back, and give production and staging different versions. LangSmith's prompt hub, Langfuse, Phoenix and AcruxCore all passed those three tests. The real differences show up elsewhere: which template syntax each one accepts, how many npm packages it pulls in, how long a change takes to reach the app, and what happens when the registry is down.

We tested all of this with one small app and one prompt, which has a condition and a loop. Git files served as the baseline. Every number below comes from a recorded run.

Most teams start with prompts in code: a template string, or a .txt file next to the code that uses it. That works until a product manager wants to change one sentence, and the change has to wait for a full release.

A prompt registry moves the text out of the deploy. The app fetches the prompt by name and by a label such as production. You change which version the label points to, and the app picks up the new text without a deploy.

LangSmith's prompt hub is the best-known registry, but it is not the only one. Several of the LangSmith alternatives also manage prompts, and three of them are open source and self-hostable: Langfuse, Phoenix and AcruxCore. We covered how those tools handle tracing in Self-Hosted LangSmith Alternatives: 4 Tools Tested on One TypeScript Agent. This article covers prompts only.

Every tool got the same prompt, support-reply. Its template takes three variables: a customer name, a plan, and a list of help articles. It has one condition: a priority line appears only for premium customers. It also has one loop: one line per help article. In Jinja2 syntax, the template looks like this:

One help article contains the text Settings > Export. We included it on purpose, to catch any tool that HTML-escapes > into >.

Every tool then ran the same sequence from a TypeScript script:

Each tool used its own documented TypeScript SDK. The four registries passed all six steps. The rest of this article covers how each one got there, and what it cost.

Keeping prompts in the repository costs almost nothing. The template sits in prompts/support-reply.njk, and nunjucks, the JavaScript port of Jinja2, renders it:

The autoescape: false line matters. Our first version called nunjucks.renderString() directly, and the test failed. The default environment HTML-escapes its output, so Settings > Export reached the model as Settings > Export. Escaping is correct for web pages and wrong for prompts.

Git gives you full Jinja2, code review on every prompt change, and history in the same place as the code. What it can't do is change a prompt in a running app. Every edit, and every rollback, waits for a deploy.

LangSmith stores a prompt as a serialized LangChain object (ChatPromptTemplate), not as plain text. LangSmith's own documentation says you must use the LangChain npm package to pull prompts in TypeScript (Manage prompts programmatically). So a plain TypeScript app that uses the openai package still needs langchain and @langchain/core:

Those three packages installed 23 packages and 70 MB. Most of the size is @langchain/* (27 MB) and js-tiktoken (22 MB).

Templates. In TypeScript, LangChain accepts f-string and mustache templates. Mustache sections ({{#docs}}…{{/docs}}) handled our loop. Mustache has no ==, though, so the premium check became an is_premium boolean computed in code. We also tried templateFormat: "jinja2", which LangChain's Python package supports. In TypeScript it threw DEFAULT_PARSER_MAPPING[templateFormat] is not a function.

Labels and rollback. production and staging are reserved tags, and the documentation moves them in the UI. The langsmith JavaScript client has no public method that moves a tag. Our script called the same REST route the SDK uses internally (POST /api/v1/repos/-//tags). It worked, but it is not a documented API.

Caching. The SDK caches prompts for 300 seconds by default. In our test, a running app saw the new version 300 seconds after we moved the tag.

Langfuse's TypeScript client is one package, @langfuse/client. It installed 5 packages and 8 MB:

Creating a version, moving a label and reading by label are all public SDK calls: prompt.create, prompt.update and prompt.get (Get started with prompt management).

Templates. The documentation shows only {{variable}} substitution. But compile() calls the mustache library, so sections worked for our loop. The type signature accepts only string values, so passing a list needed a cast. The Langfuse UI colours the section tags red, and it lists title and body as the prompt's variables, not docs or is_premium. We would not depend on behaviour the documentation doesn't promise. When we sent the Jinja2 version instead, Langfuse returned the {% if %} and {% for %} tags unchanged, with no error. The model would have received them as text.

Caching. The cache time is 60 seconds by default (Caching). Our app saw the change after 62 seconds. Langfuse is also the only client with a fallback option: a prompt string the SDK uses when it cannot reach the server on a cold start.

Phoenix's TypeScript client fetches a prompt by name and tag, and toSDK() converts it into the message format the openai package expects. Phoenix templates only replace variables, so the condition and the loop have to run in your code before rendering:

That is 16 lines, against 6 to 8 for the other registries. All the extra lines are template logic that now lives in the app.

Templates. Phoenix offers MUSTACHE, F_STRING and NONE formats (Prompts reference). Even in MUSTACHE mode, though, rendering is a plain replacement of {{name}}. When we sent mustache sections, they came back unchanged, with no error.

Errors. getPrompt() catches every error, logs it and returns null. You can see this in the client source, src/utils/getPromptBySelector.ts. A missing prompt and an unreachable server look the same to your code, which is why the snippet checks for null.

Size and caching. The client installed 57 packages and 90 MB. Most of that is OpenTelemetry, which comes in through its @arizeai/phoenix-otel dependency. The client has no prompt cache, so every render is a network call. That is also why a label change showed up immediately: 0 seconds in our test.

AcruxCore stores Jinja2 templates and renders them on the server with nunjucks. The SDK sends the variables and gets back finished messages:

The template from the test section works as it is, condition and loop included, with no is_premium flag computed in code. A label is called an alias here, and promoteAlias() moves it (Manage prompts via the SDK). The SDK installed 3 packages and 12 MB. Most of the 12 MB is one OpenTelemetry package, @opentelemetry/semantic-conventions.

Templates. Jinja2 only. When we committed the mustache version, AcruxCore rejected it at commit time with Template parse error: unexpected token: #. That is a clear error, but it also means that moving mustache prompts from LangSmith or Langfuse requires rewriting them.

We sent the same two templates, a Jinja2 one and a mustache one, to each tool's own renderer:

The rows that matter most are the ones where the tags were left in the output, because those failures produce no error. The model receives raw template syntax, and the only symptom is a strange answer. Whatever tool you pick, test one prompt with logic in it before you move the rest.

Putting logic in the template is not automatically better. It lets someone change the condition without a deploy. It also lets them break the condition without a deploy. Keeping logic in code, as Phoenix forces you to do, puts every branch under code review and in your tests.

A registry is a new runtime dependency, so we tested an outage. The app reached each registry through a local proxy, and we switched the proxy off. A separate process then started while the registry was still unreachable.

LangSmith, Langfuse and AcruxCore keep serving the last good version while the registry is down, even after the cache time has passed. The risk is a cold start: a new container or a serverless function that boots during the outage. Langfuse's fallback option is the built-in answer to that. With the other tools, keep a copy of each prompt in your code as a fallback, which brings back part of the Git setup.

Keep prompts in Git if engineers make every prompt change and your deploys are quick. Set autoescape: false, and you get Jinja2, reviews and history for free.

Use LangSmith's prompt hub if you already use LangChain, or already trace with LangSmith. If your app doesn't use LangChain, budget for the extra dependency and for the tag moves that only the UI supports.

Use Langfuse if you want a small client, a local compile(), and a fallback for cold starts. Keep to {{variable}} substitution unless you are comfortable relying on mustache behaviour that the documentation doesn't promise.

Use Phoenix if you already use it for tracing, or if you prefer all template logic in code. Remember the null check, and that every render is a network call.

Use AcruxCore if you want real conditions and loops in the template and rendering on the server. Expect one network call per new set of variables, and a personal API key for writes.

Before you choose, run one of your own prompts with real logic in it through the tool's SDK, not through its playground. Two of the four registries we tested failed on template syntax without any error, and an SDK run is where you would see that.
